Smoothness of the diffusion coefficients for particle systems in continuous space
Arianna Giunti, Chenlin Gu, Jean-Christophe Mourrat, Maximilian Nitzschner

Abstract
. For a class of particle systems in continuous space with local interactions, we show that the asymptotic diffusion matrix is an infinitely differentiable function of the density of particles. Our method allows us to identify relatively explicit descriptions of the derivatives of the diffusion matrix in terms of correctors. MSC 2010: 82C22, 35B27, 60K35. Keywords: interacting particle system, hydrodynamic limit, bulk diffusion matrix.



1.
Introduction

We study a class of interacting particle systems with local interactions in continuous space. These are systems of interacting Brownian particles, where each particle evolves in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
according to a diffusion matrix that depends on the locations of the particles nearby. The models we study are reversible with respect to the Poisson measures with constant density, uniformly elliptic, and of non-gradient type. In the limit of large scales, the evolution of the empirical distribution of particles is expected to be captured by a nonlinear diffusion equation. Such a result is usually called a
hydrodynamic limit
, and the matrix appearing in this diffusion equation is often called the
bulk diffusion matrix
. The qualifier “bulk” underlines that this matrix is meant to describe the collective evolution of the cloud of particles. We refer to the monographs
[
26
,
27
,
40
]
for thorough expositions on the topic.

The purpose of this work is to show that the bulk diffusion matrix is an infinitely differentiable function of the density of particles. That the bulk diffusion matrix is sufficiently regular as a function of the density of particles is a necessary ingredient in the proof of the hydrodynamic limit of the model, see for instance
[
20
]
. We hope that, when combined with
[
23
]
, the present work will allow for the establishment of a quantitative version of the statement of hydrodynamic limit.

Similar results on the smoothness of the effective diffusion matrix have already been derived for a number of other models of particle systems
[
12
,
14
,
29
,
30
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
41
]
. Those works all rely on the approach introduced in
[
29
]
to show the regularity of the self-diffusion matrix of a tagged particle in the symmetric simple exclusion process on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. This approach relies on certain duality properties of the process under consideration.

The approach we employ here seems different and more direct. In particular, we end up with relatively explicit expressions for the derivatives of the bulk diffusion matrix in terms of correctors, which are natural objects that already appear in the description of the bulk diffusion matrix itself.

Our method takes inspiration from works on the homogenization of elliptic equations with random coefficients
[
5
,
6
,
16
,
34
]
; see also
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
13
,
28
]
. One can for instance consider a setting in which the random coefficients of the equation are a local function of a Poisson point process with constant density, and ask whether the homogenized matrix depends smoothly on the density of the point process. This question has been answered positively in
[
15
, Theorem 5.A.1]
, following the suggestion in
[
16
, Remark 2.7]
to rely on precise quantitative estimates on the corrector and the Green function (the results of
[
16
]
require the perturbative point process to have a uniformly bounded number of points in a given bounded region of space, a property that does not hold for Poisson point processes). These precise estimates are currently not available in the context of interacting particle systems, and we show here that they are not necessary for the proof of smoothness of the homogenized coefficients.
Outside of the present paper, we are unaware of results concerning interacting particle systems for which the number of particles in a bounded region of space is not uniformly bounded.

In a previous version of this paper, we stated: “We believe that the method used here could be adapted to the case of elliptic equations and yield a simpler proof of
[
15
, Theorem 5.A.1]
that does not rely on quantitative homogenization theory.” That this is indeed the case has been rigorously established recently in
[
17
]
.

It is asserted in
[
17
]
that the problem considered here is a particular case of the corresponding problem for elliptic equations with random coefficients. This is however not the case, and indeed, the two problems have a different mathematical structure. In the context of particle systems, one
cannot
keep the set of all the other particles frozen in place, as the relevant operator must encode the movement of the entire cloud of particles at once. In other words, in the case of elliptic equations with random coefficients, for each fixed realization of the randomness, we can write down the relevant
d
-dimensional equations, which will feature random coefficients. Instead, for interacting particle systems, the relevant equations must be deterministic and
Nd
-dimensional, if the number of particles is fixed at
N
. In particular, the bulk diffusion matrix is
different
from the asymptotic diffusivity matrix of the diffusion in the random environment obtained by freezing the cloud of all the other particles.

One basic difficulty when studying homogenized coefficients is that they are defined as infinite-volume quantities. As in all other works on the topic, we will therefore start by studying a localized version of the homogenized coefficients. In the context of elliptic equations with random coefficients, this is usually achieved by the introduction of a zero-order term in the equation solved by the corrector. Through this procedure, one obtains a localized corrector that is also stationary under the action of translations.

In the context of particle systems, the correctors are deterministic functions of the cloud of particles. Stationarity thus boils down to invariance under translations, and by the ergodic theorem, a function that is invariant under translations must be constant. A different approach is therefore necessary. We will rely on the finite-volume quantities introduced in
[
23
]
, which are inspired by the analogous quantities introduced in
[
11
]
for PDEs (see also the monograph
[
9
]
, and
[
35
]
for a gentle introduction). As will be explained below, there are two such quantities; in the PDE setting, one relates to the imposition of a constant tangential gradient on the boundary, while the other relates to the imposition of a constant normal flux on the boundary. In the context of particle systems, we will rely on this second “flux” quantity for our proofs, and thus first obtain the smoothness of the inverse of the homogenized matrix. Perhaps surprisingly, significant additional technical difficulties seem to arise if one tries to devise an argument that would rely instead on the “gradient” quantity; we refer to Subsection
3.3
for more on this point.

Once our quantity is localized in finite volume, it becomes smooth and we can compute its derivatives at arbitrarily high order using classical formulas such as (
5.9
) (this formula is called a chaos expansion in
[
32
]
, and is called a cluster expansion in
[
16
]
).
The difficulty that remains is to find suitable estimates in order to pass to the limit of infinite volume. The estimates we need have a similar form as those appearing in
[
5
,
6
,
16
]
in the PDE setting; the authors of
[
16
]
also single out
[
4
, Proposition 3.4]
as an important source of inspiration. However, several adaptations are needed, due to the nature of our localization procedure as well as to the differences inherent to particle systems mentioned above; see for instance the discussion below the statement of Proposition
3.3
.

Another setting in which an expansion for the homogenized coefficients is studied are colloidal particle suspensions
[
18
,
21
,
22
,
25
,
39
]
. Under a suitable limit of many small particles, the homogenized equation for the fluid is a Stokes system having an effective viscosity. The latter admits an expansion in terms of the asymptotic volume fraction occupied by the particles.

We finally mention that questions similar to the ones contained in this paper were also investigated in the context of the
\nabla\phi
model (a Gibbs measure modeling a fluctuating interface)
[
10
]
, and non-linear elliptic equations with random coefficients
[
7
,
8
,
19
]
. In these settings, the goal is to show that the homogenized coefficients depend smoothly on the slope of the limit homogenized solution. This is not a situation in which the varying parameter can be nicely encoded by random fields with short-range correlations. As a consequence, a different, more quantitative approach is then mandatory.

2.
Precise statement of the main results

We start by introducing some notation. We view a cloud of particles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as an element of
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, the space of
\sigma
-finite measures that are sums of Dirac masses on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. The dynamics of the particles is encoded by a mapping
\mathbf{a}_{\circ}:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{sym}}
taking values in the space of symmetric
d
-by-
d
matrices. We assume that this mapping satisfies the following properties.

\bullet

Uniform ellipticity
: there exists
\Lambda<\infty
such that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}\leqslant\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu)\leqslant\Lambda\,\mathrm{Id}.

\bullet

Finite range of dependence
: for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, we have that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu)=\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}B_{1/2}),

where
B_{1/2}
denotes the Euclidean ball of unit diameter centered at the origin, and
\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}
is the restriction operator defined in (
3.1
).

In (
2.1
) and throughout the paper, whenever
a
and
b
are symmetric matrices, we write
a\leqslant b
to mean that
b-a
is a positive semidefinite matrix.

Roughly speaking, we want a particle sitting at the origin and surrounded by a cloud of particles
\mu
to undergo an instantaneous diffusion driven by the matrix
\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu)
. We extend the mapping
\mathbf{a}_{\circ}
by stationarity by setting, for every
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
,

\mathbf{a}(\mu,x):=\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\tau_{-x}\mu),

where
\tau_{-x}\mu
is the measure
\mu
translated by the vector
-x
; in other words, for every Borel set
U
, we have
\tau_{-x}\mu(U)=\mu(x+U)
.
For every
\rho_{0}\geqslant 0
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
the law of a Poisson point process over
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with constant intensity
\rho_{0}
. We denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}
the associated expectation, and use
\mu
for the canonical random variable on this probability space. The interacting particle system we aim to study is associated with the formal Dirichlet form

f\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla f\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla f\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].

We refer to (
3.3
) below for the definition of the gradient of a sufficiently smooth function defined on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and
[
24
]
for a rigorous construction of the stochastic process.

We expect the evolution of this particle system to be described by a “homogenized” or “hydrodynamic” equation over large scales. Indeed, this has been shown for discrete models similar to the continuous one studied here, see in particular
[
20
]
. In order to justify this rigorously, it is very useful to know about the regularity of the homogenized matrix, usually called the
bulk diffusion matrix
, that enters into the equation. The aim of the present work is to show that this matrix is indeed an infinitely differentiable function of the particle density.

For our purposes, it will be convenient to identify the bulk diffusion matrix as a limit of finite-volume approximations. In finite volume, there are in fact two natural approximations to the bulk diffusion matrix, which were introduced in
[
23
]
and are inspired by
[
9
,
11
,
35
]
. They are based on the following subadditive quantities: for every bounded domain
U
,
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and
\rho_{0}>0
, we define

(2.3)

\begin{split}\nu(U,p,\rho_{0})&:=\inf_{v\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}\frac{1}{2}(p+\nabla v)\cdot\mathbf{a}(p+\nabla v)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],\\
\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})&:=\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right],\end{split}

where
|U|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
U
. Recall that
\mu
is a sum of Dirac masses; for any function
F
, the integral
\int_{U}F\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu=\int_{U}F(z)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(z)
therefore stands for the summation of
F(z)
over every point
z
in the intersection of
U
and the support of
\mu
.
The precise definitions of the function spaces
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
are given in Section
3
below. Informally, the functions in
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
are those whose squared gradient have finite integral over
U
; the functions in
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
must in addition respond continuously to the exit from
U
or the entrance into
U
of a particle.
One can check (see
[
23
, Proposition 4.1]
or Subsection
3.3
below) that there exist symmetric
d
-by-
d
matrices
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U,\rho_{0}),{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U,\rho_{0})
that satisfy the bound (
2.1
) and such that, for every
p,q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,

(2.4)

\nu(U,p,\rho_{0})=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(U,\rho_{0})p\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U,\rho_{0})q.

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}:=(-3^{m}/2,3^{m}/2)^{d}
the cube of side-length
3^{m}
centered at the origin. We also have that the sequence
({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is decreasing, and the sequence
({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing. We define the bulk diffusion matrix as the limit of the latter sequence:

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho_{0}):=\lim_{m\to\infty}{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0}).

It was shown in
[
23
]
that the sequence
({\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0}))_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to the same limit, and moreover, that there exists an exponent
\alpha>0
and a constant
C<\infty
such that for every
m\geqslant 1
,

(2.6)

\left|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0})-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho_{0})\right|+\left|{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0})-{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho_{0})\right|\leqslant C3^{-\alpha m}.

The results of the present paper do not rely on this quantitative information. Indeed, to show our main results, we only appeal to (
2.5
) as the definition of the limit diffusion matrix. This definition coincides with the more classical one based on full-space stationary correctors, as explained in
[
23
, Appendix B]
.

Throughout the paper, we fix
q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, and denote by
\psi_{m}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
the optimizer in the definition of
\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})
, see (
2.3
). The optimizer for
\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})
is unique provided that we impose the condition in (
3.9
) (the formulas derived throughout the paper only involve gradients of
\psi_{m}
, and are therefore insensitive to the precise way we “fix the constants”).

For reasons that will be clarified below, we prefer to work with
\psi_{m}
, which optimizes some
\nu_{*}
quantity, rather than with the corresponding optimizer for
\nu
. One consequence of this choice is that we have easier access to information about the smoothness of the mapping
\rho\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\rho)
than of the mapping
\rho\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho)
. Of course, since these matrices are uniformly elliptic in the sense of (
2.1
), discussing the smoothness of one or the other is equivalent (and from a physical perspective, it is no less natural to focus on “fixing the average flux at
q
” than to focus on “fixing the average gradient at
p
”).

For clarity of exposition, we will first present a proof that the mapping
\rho\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\rho)
is
C^{1,1}
. The precise statement is as follows.

Theorem 2.1

(
C^{1,1}
regularity)
.

The following limit is well-defined and finite

(2.7)

c_{1}(\rho_{0}):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla\psi_{m}\cdot(\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{a}^{\{1\}})\nabla\psi_{m}^{\{1\}}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1},

where we write
\mathbf{a}^{\{1\}}(\mu,z,x_{1}):=\mathbf{a}\left(\mu+\delta_{x_{1}},z\right)
and
\nabla\psi_{m}^{\{1\}}(\mu,z,x_{1}):=\nabla\psi_{m}\left(\mu+\delta_{x_{1}},z\right)
. Moreover, as
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
tends to zero, we have

(2.8)

q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\rho_{0}+\rho)q=q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\rho_{0})q+\rho c_{1}(\rho_{0})+O(\rho^{2}).

The term
O(\rho^{2})
hides a multiplicative constant that depends only on
d
,
\Lambda
and
|q|
(but not on
\rho_{0}
).

Remark 2.2
.

Theorem
2.1
yields that
q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\cdot)q
is
C^{1,1}
. Indeed, an immediate consequence of expansion (
2.8
) is that
c_{1}(\cdot)
is the derivative of
q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\cdot)q
. Moreover, using (
2.8
) around
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{0}+\rho
, we see that
c_{1}(\rho_{0}+\rho)=c_{1}(\rho_{0})+O(\rho)
, i.e. that
c_{1}
is Lipschitz continuous.

A more explicit writing of the right side of (
2.7
) is:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla\psi_{m}(\mu,z)\cdot(\mathbf{a}(\mu,z)-\mathbf{a}\left(\mu+\delta_{x_{1}},z\right))\nabla\psi_{m}(\mu+\delta_{x_{1}},z)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(z)\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}.

In general, we use superscripts to indicate changes in the “measure” argument of the function under consideration: for instance, the quantity
\mathbf{a}^{\{1\}}
is obtained from
\mathbf{a}
by replacing the argument
\mu
with
\mu+\delta_{x_{1}}
.

In order to describe higher-order derivatives, we need to generalize this notation to arbitrary subsets of indices. For every finite subset
E\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
f
an arbitrary function of the measure
\mu
, we define

(2.9)

f^{E}:(\mu,(x_{i})_{i\in E})\mapsto f(\mu+\sum_{i\in E}\delta_{x_{i}}).

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we also write

(2.10)

D_{i}f:=f^{\{i\}}-f.

Notice that for every
i\neq j\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we have

D_{i}D_{j}f=(f^{\{j\}}-f)^{\{i\}}-(f^{\{i\}}-f)=f^{\{i,j\}}-f^{\{i\}}-f^{\{j\}}+f.

In particular, the operators
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
commute. We can therefore define, for every
E=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{p}\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
, the quantity

(2.11)

D_{E}f:=D_{i_{1}}\cdots D_{i_{p}}f.

Finally, we need at times to apply these operators to more complex expressions such as
f+g
, where
f
and
g
are two functions of the measure
\mu
, with the understanding that the operator applies only to
f
and not to
g
. We use the superscript
\#
to indicate the functions on which these operators are meant to be applied, keeping the others “frozen”. That is, we write for instance

\displaystyle(f^{\#}+g)^{E}=f^{E}+g,\qquad(f^{\#}g)^{E}=f^{E}g,

and similarly with more complex expressions.
We also have

\displaystyle D_{E}(f^{\#}+g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f+g,&\text{ if }E=\emptyset,\\
D_{E}f,&\text{ if }E\neq\emptyset,\end{array}\right.\qquad D_{E}(f^{\#}g)=(D_{E}f)g.

We use the notation
[\![1,k]\!]:=\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Here is our main result.

Theorem 2.3

(Smoothness)
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, there exists a constant
C_{k}(\Lambda,d)<\infty
(not depending on
\rho_{0}
) such that the limit

(2.12)

c_{k}(\rho_{0})\\
:=\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{k}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\bigg[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\nabla\psi_{m}\cdot D_{[\![1,k]\!]}\left((\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{a}^{\#})\nabla\psi^{\#}_{m}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\,\cdots\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{k},

is well-defined and satisfies
|c_{k}(\rho_{0})|\leqslant C_{k}|q|^{2}
. Moreover, the mapping
\rho_{0}\mapsto q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}^{-1}(\rho_{0})q
is infinitely differentiable, and its
k
-th derivative is
c_{k}(\rho_{0})
.

Note that due to the local nature of the term
(\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{a}^{\#})
, see (
2.2
), the integrals in (
2.7
) and (
2.12
) are in fact finite-volume quantities, as the outermost integrals may be replaced by, for instance,
\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1}}
and
\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{k}}
, respectively.

If one modifies the unit range of dependence assumption in (
2.2
), assuming instead that there exists
R\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
,

(2.13)

\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu)=\mathbf{a}_{\circ}(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}B_{R/2}),

then the fact that the mapping
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}
depends smoothly on the density
\rho_{0}
can be obtained by a change of scale. The particle density for the rescaled process is
R^{d}\rho_{0}
. Since the constants in Theorem
2.3
do not depend on
\rho_{0}
, we obtain that, under the assumption of (
2.13
) and with the same definition (
2.12
) of
c_{k}(\rho_{0})
, we have

|c_{k}(\rho_{0})|\leqslant C_{k}|q|^{2}\,R^{kd}.

Estimates of this type may be useful for controlling situations in which the range of dependence is unbounded.

We now comment on the reason why we choose to work with quantities derived from
\nu_{*}
rather than
\nu
. Recall that the function
\psi_{m}
is the optimizer in the definition (
2.3
) of
\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})
. This object may seem to depend upon the choice of the particle density
\rho_{0}
. However, it is in fact not the case. Indeed, the optimization problem for
\nu_{*}
can be split into a sum of unrelated optimization problems, one for each fixed number of particles in
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
. The optimizer for
\nu_{*}
is thus a superposition of these optimizers, irrespectively of the underlying density of the measure.
We refer to Section
3
below for a more detailed discussion of this property. The fact that we can view the same object
\psi_{m}
as the optimizer of
\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})
for arbitrary values of
\rho_{0}
would not be valid were we to work with the optimizers of
\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p,\rho_{0})
.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. We discuss function spaces more precisely in Section
3
, and prove a technically useful lemma stating that the quantity
\nu_{*}
does not change if the particles become distinguishable. We then show Theorem
2.1
in Section
4
. The more general Theorem
2.3
is then proved in Section
5
. Finally, in Section
6
, we show that the mappings
\rho_{0}\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0})
and
\rho_{0}\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\rho_{0})
converge to
\rho_{0}\mapsto{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho_{0})
locally uniformly, and that this is also the case for the convergence in (
2.12
) towards the higher-order derivatives of
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}(\rho_{0})
.

3.
Setting and functional framework

In this section, we rigorously introduce the notation and functional framework that we use in this paper. In particular, we define the function spaces
\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
that appear in the optimization problems
\nu
and
\nu_{*}
in (
2.3
). This will also allow us to justify why, as mentioned in the previous section, we will prove the main results of this paper by mainly working with the quantity
\nu_{*}
instead of
\nu
.

3.1.
Configuration space

We denote by
\mathbb{R}^{d}
the standard Euclidean space, by
Q_{s}:=\left(-s/2,s/2\right)^{d}
the open hypercube of side length
s>0
, and we write
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}:=Q_{3^{m}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We also use

\textstyle\square

as a shorthand notation for the unit cube
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{0}
.

We recall that
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
is the space of
\sigma
-finite measures that are sums of Dirac masses on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, which we think of as the configuration space of particles, and that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
corresponds to the probability measure for the Poisson point process having constant density
\rho_{0}>0
. We write
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}
for the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
. For a Borel set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{U}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the mappings
\mu\mapsto\mu(V)
, for all Borel sets
V\subseteq U
, completed with all the
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-null sets. We use the notation
\mathcal{F}
for
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}
. With this construction, assumption (
2.2
) yields that the random matrix
\mathbf{a}_{\circ}:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{sym}}
is an
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1/2}}
-measurable mapping.

3.2.
Function spaces

We now introduce several function spaces on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
that will be used in this paper. In particular, we will give the rigorous definition of
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
.

We start with basic considerations concerning
\mathcal{F}
-measurable functions on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. Given a Borel set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, it is often useful to decompose an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function into a series of Borel-measurable functions on Euclidean spaces, conditioned on the number of particles in
U
and the configuration outside
U
. More precisely, we denote by
\mathcal{B}_{U}
the set of Borel subsets of
U
. For every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, we denote by
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
the measure such that, for every Borel set
V\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,

(3.1)

(\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U)(V)=\mu(U\cap V).

Then for
f:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}
which is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, we define

(3.2)

f_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}):\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}U^{n}&\to&\mathbb{R}\\
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\mapsto&f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}\right).\end{array}\right.

The function
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{B}_{U}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{U^{c}}
-measurable. Reciprocally, given a series of permutation-invariant functions with such measurability properties, we can reconstruct an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function
f
by specifying that, on the event
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
, we have
f(\mu):=f_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
. We call the mapping
f\mapsto(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the “canonical projection”, and refer to
[
23
, Lemmas 2.2 and A.1]
for more details.

We now explain the notion of derivatives for functions defined on
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. For every sufficiently smooth function
f:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and
x\in\supp\mu
, the gradient
\nabla f(\mu,x)
is such that, for every
{k\in\{1,\ldots,d\}}
,

(3.3)

\mathrm{e}_{k}\cdot\nabla f(\mu,x)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{f(\mu-\delta_{x}+\delta_{x+h\mathrm{e}_{k}})-f(\mu)}{h},

where
(\mathrm{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{e}_{d})
is the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. While we wish to emphasize that the function
\nabla f(\mu,\cdot)
is naturally defined only on
\supp\mu
, we extend it for convenience as

(3.4)

\text{for every }x\notin\supp\mu,\quad\nabla f(\mu,x):=0.

To clarify the notion of smooth functions appearing in the previous paragraph, we can appeal to the canonical projections discussed above. For every bounded open set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we define the sets of smooth functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)
in the following way. We have that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
if and only if
f
is an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable function, and for every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
f_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
appearing in (
3.2
) is infinitely differentiable on
U^{n}
.
The space
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)
is the subspace of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
of functions that are
\mathcal{F}_{K}
-measurable for some compact set
K\subseteq U
.

We define
\mathscr{L}^{2}
to be the space of
\mathcal{F}
-measurable functions
f
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[f^{2}\right]
is finite. As usual, elements in this function space that coincide
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost surely are identified. We now define
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
as the infinite-dimensional analogue of the classical Sobolev space
H^{1}
: for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
, we introduce the norm

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}=\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[f^{2}(\mu)]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{U}|\nabla f(\mu,x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu(x)\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and set

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)\,\colon\,\left\|{f}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}<+\infty\}}^{\left\|{\,\cdot\,}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)\,\colon\,\left\|{f}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}<+\infty\}}^{\left\|{\,\cdot\,}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}},

namely the completion, under
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}
, of the sets of functions in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
or
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)
that have finite norm
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}
. As for classical Sobolev spaces, for every
f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
, we can interpret
\nabla f(\mu,x)
when
x\in U\cap\supp\mu
in a weak sense. This may be understood via the canonical projection in (
3.2
).

The two spaces
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
share many similarities as well as some fundamental differences. The latter ones, in turn, derive from the differences between
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)
: On the one hand, functions in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
do depend on
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
and the number of particles
\mu(U)
in a relatively arbitrary (measurable) way. On the other hand, functions in the subset
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U)
are
\mathcal{F}_{U}
-measurable as they do not depend on particles that cross the boundary
\partial U
.

When managing elements in
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
or
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
, it is at times useful to think about them in terms of their canonical projection defined in (
3.2
): Let
f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
and let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the associated canonical projection. Then for
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost every
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

•

The function
f_{n}(\,\cdot\,,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
belongs to the (standard) Sobolev space
H^{1}(U^{n})
;

•

The function
f_{n}(\,\cdot\,,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
is invariant under permutations: if
S_{n}
denotes the set of permutations of
[\![1,n]\!]
and we write
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in U^{n}
, then for every
\sigma\in S_{n}
it holds

(3.7)

\displaystyle f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})=f_{n}(x_{\sigma(1)},\cdots x_{\sigma(n)},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\ \ \ \ \text{almost everywhere in $U^{n}$.}

If
f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
, then the canonical partition needs to satisfy the following additional “compatibility condition”: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and on the set
\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in U^{n}\,\colon\,x_{1}\in\partial U\}
, it holds

(3.8)

\displaystyle f_{n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})=f_{n-1}(x_{2},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}),

where the identity is to be understood in the sense of traces. Note that, by the invariance under permutations, the above property also holds if
x_{1}
is replaced by any other coordinate
x_{i}
,
i=2,\cdots,n
. Moreover, for every
f\in\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}(U)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
f_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
in fact does not depend on
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
.

We summarize the previous remarks in Table
3.1
.

.

3.3.
Elementary properties of optimizers

As seen in the previous subsection, the spaces
\mathscr{H}^{1}
and
\mathscr{H}^{1}_{0}
differ in important ways, and this will translate into differences for the optimizers of
\nu
and
\nu_{*}
. In fact, except in part of Section
6
, we will only rely on quantities derived from
\nu_{*}
. In this subsection, we present some key properties of optimizers of this quantity, and highlight those that would not be shared by the optimizers of
\nu
.

For
U
a Lipschitz domain and
q\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we denote by
\psi_{U,q}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
the maximizer in the definition of
\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})
. By
[
23
, Proposition 4.1]
(see also Lemma
3.1
below), this optimizer exists, and is unique provided we also impose that

(3.9)

{\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{U,q}\ \big|\ \mu(U),\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}\right]=0}.

This optimizer is
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1/2}(U)}
-measurable, with
B_{1/2}(U)=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}:\dist(x,U)<\frac{1}{2}\}
. Since
q\mapsto\psi_{U,q}
is a linear mapping, there exists a matrix
{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U,\rho_{0})
such that

\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\psi_{U,q}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\psi_{U,q}+q\cdot\nabla\psi_{U,q}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U,\rho_{0})q.

The uniform ellipticity assumption (
2.1
) readily implies that
\mathrm{Id}\leqslant{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}(U,\rho_{0})\leqslant\Lambda\mathrm{Id}
.
By the first variation, we have for every
u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
that

(3.10)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{U}\left(-\nabla\psi_{U,q}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0.

Using this with
u=\psi_{U,q}
, we get that

(3.11)

\begin{split}q\cdot{\overbracket{\mathbf{a}}}_{*}^{-1}(U,\rho_{0})q&=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}\nabla\psi_{U,q}\cdot\mathbf{a}\nabla\psi_{U,q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}q\cdot\nabla\psi_{U,q}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right].\end{split}

In particular, using the uniform ellipticity assumption once more, we obtain the basic Dirichlet energy estimate

(3.12)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}|\nabla\psi_{U,q}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]\leqslant|q|^{2}.

Similar properties are also valid for optimizers of
\nu
, and we refer to
[
23
, Proposition 4.1]
for details. Optimizers of
\nu_{*}
differ however in one crucial aspect: denoting by
(\psi_{U,q,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the canonical projection of
\psi_{U,q}
, see (
3.2
), we can identify each
\psi_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
as the solution to an elliptic equation. In particular, the function
\psi_{U,q}
, which was defined as the optimizer in the definition of
\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})
,
in fact does not depend on
\rho_{0}
. This property would not be valid for optimizers of
\nu
.

In order to clarify this, we introduce the following notation: for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, and
u\in H^{1}(U^{n})
, we write

(3.13)

\mathcal{J}_{n}(u,U,q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\\
:=\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\strokedint_{U^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{x_{i}}u\cdot\mathbf{a}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c},x_{i}\right)\nabla_{x_{i}}u+q\cdot\nabla_{x_{i}}u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\cdots\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{n}.

This quantity corresponds to the functional that is optimized in the definition of
\nu_{*}
, see (
2.3
), but where we have conditioned on
\mu(U)=n
and
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
; and where moreover, we substituted an arbitrary
u\in H^{1}(U^{n})
in place of the canonical projection
u_{n}
of some function
u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
. We thus have

\displaystyle\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mu(U)=n\right]\,\mathcal{J}_{n}(u_{n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}),U,q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\right]

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mu(U)=n\right]\,\sup_{u\in H^{1}(U^{n})}\mathcal{J}_{n}(u,U,q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\right].

The next lemma implies that the inequality above is in fact an equality. Recall that we denote by
(\psi_{U,q,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the canonical projection of
\psi_{U,q}
, the optimizer of
\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\in H^{1}(U^{n})
be the unique maximizer of the functional
\mathcal{J}_{n}(\cdot,U,q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
subject to the constraint
\strokedint_{U^{n}}u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})=0
. For
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(3.15)

u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})=\psi_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}).

Remark 3.2
.

The quantities
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
and
\psi_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
in fact only depend on the restriction of
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
to the set of points that are at distance at most
1/2
from
U
, by the finite-range dependence assumption (
2.2
). The statement that (
3.15
) holds for
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
therefore does not depend on
\rho_{0}>0
. We are forced to state (
3.15
) only for
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost every
\mu
since a priori we only know that
\psi_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
is well-defined for
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
-almost every
\mu
; but the lemma itself provides us with a straightforward way to extend the definition to every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
. In the proof below, we observe that there exists a function
u_{U,q}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
whose canonical projection is
(u_{U,q,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and then show that
u_{U,q}=\psi_{U,q}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

We first observe that, for each
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{\delta}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})
, the function
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
is invariant under permutation of its coordinates. This is immediate from the facts that
\mathcal{J}_{n}(\cdot,U,q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
admits a unique mean-zero maximizer, and that this functional as well as the mean-zero constraint are invariant under permutations.

We now define the function
u_{U,q}:\mathcal{M}_{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
in such a way that, on the event that
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}
, we have

\displaystyle u_{U,q}(\mu):=u_{U,q,n}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}).

This definition makes sense since we have verified that
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
is invariant under permutation of its coordinates. It is also clear that the canonical projection of the function
u_{U,q}
is the family of functions
(u_{U,q,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(so the notation is sound).

We now argue that
u_{U,q}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})
, and by the uniqueness of the optimizer for
\nu_{*}
and (
3.14
), this will imply that
u_{U,q}=\psi_{U,q}
, as desired. Let now
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
be fixed. By construction, each function
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
satisfies, for every
v\in H^{1}(U^{n})
, the variational identity

\displaystyle\int_{U^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\nabla_{x_{i}}u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})\cdot\mathbf{a}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{x_{i}}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c},x_{i}\right)\nabla_{x_{i}}v-q\cdot\nabla_{x_{i}}v\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}\cdots\,{\mathrm{d}}x_{n}=0.

Choosing
v=u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
and using (
2.1
) and Young’s inequality, we infer that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\strokedint_{U^{n}}|\nabla_{x_{i}}u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})|^{2}\leqslant|q|^{2}.

Moreover, since
u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})
has zero-average on
U^{n}
, we may apply Poincaré’s inequality in the product domain
U^{n}
(see for instance
[
33
]
or
[
23
, Proposition 3.1]
) and obtain that there exists a constant
C(U)<+\infty
such that

(3.17)

\displaystyle\strokedint_{U^{n}}|u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})|^{2}\leqslant C\sum_{i=1}^{n}\strokedint_{U^{n}}|\nabla_{x_{i}}u_{U,q,n}(\cdot,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c})|^{2}\mathrel{{\mathop{\leqslant}\limits}}Cn|q|^{2}.

Estimates (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) and the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\,\cdot\,\right]
immediately imply that
u_{u,q}\in\mathscr{H}^{1}(U)
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

As announced, Lemma
3.1
demonstrates that the optimizer for
\nu_{*}(U,q,\rho_{0})
in fact does not depend on
\rho_{0}
: regardless of the density, it is always the same
\psi_{U,q}
whose canonical projections are described by this lemma. The only difference is that optimizers for
\nu_{*}(U,q,\cdot)
at different densities receive point processes with different densities as their argument.

We also stress that another immediate consequence of Lemma
3.1
, see also (
3.16
), is that each maximizer
\psi_{U,q}
satisfies the following improved energy inequality:

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|U|}\int_{U}|\nabla\psi_{U,q}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\biggl|\,\mu(U),\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}\right]\leqslant|q|^{2}\frac{\mu(U)}{\rho_{0}|U|},

for every
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}U^{c}
and number of particles
\mu(U)\in\mathbb{N}
fixed. Note that this inequality implies (
3.12
).

In most of the paper, we keep the parameter
q
fixed, and work with the domain
U={\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
. We recall the notation
\psi_{m}:=\psi_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q}
.

3.4.
Coupling of point processes

When studying the regularity of the bulk diffusion matrix, it is useful to introduce a coupling between different densities. Recall that we keep
\rho_{0}\in(0,\infty)
fixed, and let
\mu\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\rho_{0})
be the “reference” Poisson point process, with constant density
\rho_{0}
. For
\rho\geqslant 0
, we define another independent Poisson point process
\mu_{\rho}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\rho)
, which we think of as a small perturbation. Then we denote by
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
the joint probability measure, with associated expectation
\mathbb{E}
, and we observe that
{\mu+\mu_{\rho}\sim\mathrm{Poisson}(\rho_{0}+\rho)}
, by the superposition property for independent Poisson point processes.

Notice that the definition of the space
\mathscr{H}^{1}
actually depends on the density of particles, although this was kept implicit in the notation. When we want to resolve the ambiguity, we write
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U,\mu)

for the space as defined in (
3.5
), and we write
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U,\mu+\mu_{\rho})
for the same space but with density
(\rho_{0}+\rho)
. In line with the notation introduced in (
2.9
), we use a superscript
\rho
to indicate when the measure argument of a function is taken to be
\mu+\mu_{\rho}
. For instance, when we write
\mathbf{a}^{\rho}
in some expression, we always understand that it is evaluated as
\mathbf{a}(\mu+\mu_{\rho},\cdot)
; the notation
\mathbf{a}
is understood to be evaluated at
\mu
instead. The same convention applies as well to
\psi_{m}
and
\psi_{m}^{\rho}
: the former represents
\psi_{m}(\mu)
and the latter
\psi_{m}(\mu+\mu_{\rho})
. As discussed in the previous subsection, the function
\psi_{m}^{\rho}
can be interpreted as the optimizer of
\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0}+\rho)
. This notation allows us to write, for instance,

\displaystyle\nu_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0}+\rho)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{(\rho_{0}+\rho)|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla\psi^{\rho}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{\rho}\nabla\psi^{\rho}_{m}+q\cdot\nabla\psi^{\rho}_{m}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu+\mu_{\rho})\right].

We can also define a quantity
\nu_{*}
perturbed by adding a finite number of particles uniformly. We denote by
E\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
the index set, and write
\mu_{E}:=\sum_{i\in E}\delta_{x_{i}}
. Throughout the paper we use the following compact notation for the integration with respect to the particles in
E
:

(3.19)

\displaystyle\int_{U^{E}}(\cdots):=\int_{U^{|E|}}(\cdots)\,\prod_{i\in E}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i},\qquad\strokedint_{U^{E}}(\cdots):=\frac{1}{|U|^{|E|}}\int_{U^{|E|}}(\cdots)\,\prod_{i\in E}{\mathrm{d}}x_{i},

with the understanding that, if
E=\emptyset
, then
\strokedint_{U^{\emptyset}}(\cdots)=(\cdots)
.

We define the function space
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U,\mu+\mu_{E})
as the completion in
\mathscr{H}^{1}(U,\mu+\mu_{E})
of the space of functions in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(U)
such that the norm

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|^{2}_{\mathscr{H}^{1}(U,\mu+\mu_{E})}:=\int_{U^{E}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[f^{2}(\mu+\mu_{E})\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{U}|\nabla f(\mu+\mu_{E},x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu+\mu_{E})(x)\right]\right),

is finite. Similarly to the notation
\mathbf{a}^{\rho}
discussed above, we use the shorthand notation
\mathbf{a}^{E}
to denote the function
\mathbf{a}(\mu+\mu_{E},\cdot)
. The dual problem
\nu^{E}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})
is defined as

(3.20)

\nu^{E}_{*}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\rho_{0})\\
:=\sup_{u\in\mathscr{H}^{1}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\mu+\mu_{E})}\strokedint_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{\rho_{0}|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\nabla u\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla u+q\cdot\nabla u\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu+\mu_{E})\right],

and we denote its optimizer by
\psi^{E}_{m}
. Similarly to what was discussed for
\psi_{m}^{\rho}
in the previous subsection, we have that
\psi^{E}_{m}
coincides with the function
\psi_{m}(\mu+\mu_{E})
, and we can always think of the superscript
E
as indicating the operation of adding
\mu_{E}
to the argument of the function, see (
2.9
).

We have built the configuration space in order to capture the notion of indistinguishable particles: if we exchange the positions of two particles, the measure does not change. However, when perturbing the measure
\mu
with the addition of
\mu_{\rho}
(or
\mu_{E}
), the setting naturally introduces some amount of distinguishability between particles, as some come from the measure
\mu
and some from the measure
\mu_{\rho}
(or
\mu_{E}
). Lemma
3.1
has clarified in particular that “nothing is gained” in the optimization problem if we allow the particles to be distinguishable. We now “project” this statement into a form in which, roughly speaking, we can only distinguish from which measure (such as
\mu
,
\mu_{\rho}
or
\mu_{E}
) a particle “comes”.

Proposition 3.3
.

For all finite sets
E,F\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{+}
, we have that

(3.21)

\displaystyle\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E\cup F}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0,

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E\cup F}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{\rho,E}\nabla\psi^{\rho,E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\right]=0.

Before turning to the proof, we point out some possibly surprising features of this result. First, as pointed out above, these relations differ from (
3.10
) in that the test functions can distinguish between different types of particles: for instance, the function
\psi_{m}^{F}
depends only on
\mu+\mu_{F}
, and cannot be thought of as a function of
\mu+\mu_{\rho}+\mu_{E}
, as one might hope at first. Second, the integration of the additional particles indexed by
E\cup F
is carried over the larger domain
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1}
, instead of the domain
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
that one might expect. And finally, we integrate over
\mu
only, while one might at first expect (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) to be integrated against
\mu+\mu_{E}
and
\mu+\mu_{\rho}+\mu_{E}
respectively. The proof below will need to address each of these aspects. The particular form of (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) we have chosen here will turn out to be the most convenient later on: for instance, we will often need to study linear combinations of
\psi_{m}^{E}
’s for different sets
E
, and it is most convenient that the measure against which we integrate does not depend on
E
. Similarly, when we study the effect of a change in the density, some additional particles that fall in a layer around
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
will need to be taken into account, and it is more convenient that (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) take such perturbations into account.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

We first show (
3.21
). The proof can be divided into 4 steps.

Step 1: Decomposition.

For
E,F\subseteq\mathbb{N}
fixed, we split
E\cup F=E\sqcup(F\setminus E)
and write
\mu_{F}=\mu_{F\cap E}+\mu_{F\setminus E}
. By Fubini’s theorem we reorganize

(3.23)

\displaystyle\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E\cup F}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\biggr]

\displaystyle=\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{F\setminus E}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}[\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n]\,A_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E})\right],

where for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we defined

\displaystyle A_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E})

\displaystyle:=\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E}}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\biggl|\,\mu_{\rho},\,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}},\,\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n\right].

We note that only
\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}
depends on the realization of
\mu_{\rho}
. Hence, the previous term can be rewritten as

\displaystyle A_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E})

\displaystyle=\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\biggl|\,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}},\,\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n\right],

in which the measures
\mu_{\rho}
and
\mu_{F\setminus E}
in
\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}
are fixed.

We now apply a further decomposition of
A_{n}
. Let
G\subseteq E
be the set of particles in
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
, then the integration becomes

\displaystyle\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1})^{E}}=\sum_{G\subseteq E}\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1}\setminus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{E\setminus G}}\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{G}},

and we can write

(3.24)

A_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E})\\
=\sum_{G\subseteq E}\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1}\setminus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{E\setminus G}}B_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G}),

where the quantity
B_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})
is defined as

B_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})\\
:=\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{G}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu\,\biggl|\,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}},\,\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n\right].

Step 2: Finding the associated variational problem.

We now claim that, for each
G\subseteq E
and every
\mu_{E\setminus G}
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle B_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})=0.

To prove this, we begin by specifying where the functions
\psi_{m}^{E}
and
\mathbf{a}^{E}
are evaluated. Splitting
\mu_{E}=\mu_{E\cap G}+\mu_{E\setminus G}
and recalling the definition (
3.2
) of the canonical projection for
\psi_{m}
, we note that the term
\psi_{m}^{E}
in the expectation corresponds to
\psi_{m,n+|G|}(\,\cdot\,,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{E\setminus G})
. By Lemma
3.1
, this function is a maximizer for the functional
\mathcal{J}_{n+|G|}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{E\setminus G})
. Moreover, we notice that the left-hand side of (
3.25
) is quite similar to the variational formulation for the optimization problem for
\mathcal{J}_{n+|G|}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{E\setminus G})
that is tested against the function
\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}
, so we define

\widetilde{B}_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})\\
:=\int_{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{G}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\int_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}}(\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot\mathbf{a}^{E}\nabla\psi^{E}_{m}-\nabla\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}\cdot q)\,{\mathrm{d}}(\mu+\mu_{G})\,\biggl|\,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}},\,\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n\right].

In the following, we will

•

verify that
\psi_{m}^{\rho,F}
is an admissible test function for the optimization problem for
\mathcal{J}_{n+|G|}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{E\setminus G})
, showing that
\widetilde{B}_{n}=0
;

•

deduce from
\widetilde{B}_{n}=0
the claim (
3.25
).

Step 3: The test function is admissible.

Conditioned on
\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n
, we write
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
and
\mu_{F}
as

\displaystyle\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{i}},\qquad\mu_{F}=\sum_{i=1}^{|F\cap G|}\delta_{x_{\alpha_{i}}}+\sum_{j=1}^{|F\setminus G|}\delta_{x_{\beta_{j}}}.

Then conditioned on
\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})=n
, for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every realization of
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{\rho}
, and (Lebesgue-) almost every realization of
\mu_{F\setminus E}
, the function

\displaystyle(y_{1},\cdots,y_{n},x_{\alpha_{1}},\cdots,x_{\alpha_{|F\cap G|}})\mapsto\psi_{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{|F\cap G|}\delta_{x_{\alpha_{i}}}+\mu_{F\setminus G}+\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{\rho}\right),

belongs to
H^{1}(({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{n+|F\cap G|})
thanks to (
3.18
). Thus it also belongs to
H^{1}(({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{n+|G|})
with respect to the integration
(\mu+\mu_{G})
, and it is an admissible function for the optimization problem for
\mathcal{J}_{n+|G|}(\cdot,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q,\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c}+\mu_{E\setminus G})
. This implies that for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every realization of
\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{\rho}
, and Lebesgue-almost every realization of
\mu_{F\setminus E}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})=0.

Step 4: Passage from
\widetilde{B}_{n}=0
to
B_{n}=0
.

We stress that from the gradient of
\psi^{\rho,F}_{m}
in
\widetilde{B}_{n}
, out of the
(n+|G|)
particles in
(\mu+\mu_{G})
only those in the support of
(\mu+\mu_{F\cap G})
contribute. Thus we can rewrite
\widetilde{B}_{n}
as

\widetilde{B}_{n}(\mu_{\rho},\mu\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{c},\mu_{F\setminus E},\mu_{E\setminus G})\\
